and that
\theta/N\to\alpha
and
\xi_{0}^{N}\to\xi_{0}
weakly as
N\to\infty
,
where each
\xi_{0}^{N}
is conditionally uniform given
\eta_{0}^{N}

in the sense of (
5.12
)
and
\xi_{0}
is conditionally Poisson given
\eta_{0}

in the sense of (
5.13
).
Then for any
T
there exists a constant
K(T)
such that for all
M>0
,

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\langle h,\xi^{N}_{t}\rangle>M\right\}<\frac{K(T)}{M}.

We postpone the proof of this Lemma until we have shown how it yields compact containment.
First, we show that this implies compact containment of the processes
(\langle f,\xi^{N}_{t}\rangle)_{0\leq t\leq T}

for arbitrary compactly supported
f
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Suppose
f\in C(\overline{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times[0,\infty))
and there is a
u_{f}
such that
if
u\geq u_{f}
then
\sup_{x}f(x,u)=0
.
Under the assumptions of Lemma
8.2
,
for any
T
there exists a constant
K(f,T)
such that for all
M>0
,

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\langle f,\xi^{N}_{t}\rangle>M\right\}<\frac{K(f,T)}{M}.

Proof of Lemma
8.3
:

Let
h
be as in Lemma
8.2
,
so there is a
c_{f}<\infty
such that
f(x,u)\leq c_{f}h(u)
for all
x
and
u
.
Therefore,
\langle f,\xi\rangle\leq c_{f}\langle h,\xi\rangle
,
and so by Lemma
8.2
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\langle f,\xi^{N}_{t}\rangle>M\right\}\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\langle h,\xi^{N}_{t}\rangle>M/c_{f}\right\}<\frac{K(T)c_{f}}{M}.

Lemma 8.4
(Compact containment for
\xi
).

Let
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots
be a sequence of functions each satisfying the conditions
of Lemma
8.3
.
Under the assumptions of Lemma
8.2
,
for any
T
and
\delta>0
there exists a sequence

(C_{1},C_{2},\ldots)
of finite constants such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\langle f_{k},\xi^{N}_{t}\rangle>C_{k}\text{ for some }k\geq 1\right\}<\delta.

(8.4)

In other words, the processes
\xi^{N}
stay in
the set

\left\{\xi\in\mathcal{M}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\times[0,\infty))\;:\;\langle f_{k},\xi\rangle\leq C_{k}\text{ for all }k\geq 1\right\},

for all
0\leq t\leq T

with uniformly high probability,
a set which (as discussed in the proof of Theorem
5.4
)
is relatively compact for an appropriate choice of
\{f_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
.

Proof of Lemma
8.4
:

By a union bound,

\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\langle f_{k},\xi^{N}_{t}\rangle>C_{k}\text{ for some }k\geq 1\right\}\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\langle f_{k},\xi^{N}_{t}\rangle>C_{k}\right\},

so (
8.4
) follows
by taking
C_{k}=2^{k-1}K(f_{k},T)/\delta
and using Lemma
8.3
.

Finally, we prove the key lemma.

Proof of Lemma
8.2
:

Applied to
f(x,u)=h(u)
, the martingale representation (
8.2
) is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle h,\xi_{t}^{N}\rangle&=\langle h,\xi_{0}^{N}\rangle+M^{h}_{t}\\
&\qquad{}+\int_{0}^{t}\big\langle 2c_{\theta}(x,\eta^{N}_{s})\int_{u}^{N}h(v)dv,\xi_{s}^{N}(dx,du)\big\rangle ds\\
&\qquad\qquad{}+\int_{0}^{t}\big\langle\left(c_{\theta}(x,\eta^{N}_{s})u^{2}-b_{\theta}(x,\eta^{N}_{s})u\right)h^{\prime}(u),\xi_{s}^{N}(dx,du)\big\rangle ds,\end{split}

where
M^{h}_{t}
is a martingale
with angle bracket process

\displaystyle\left\langle M^{h}\right\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\langle 2c_{\theta}(x,\eta_{s}^{N})\int_{u}^{N}h(v)^{2}dv,\xi_{s}^{N}(dx,du)\big\rangle ds.

Now, note that
0\leq c_{\theta}(x,\eta_{x}^{N})\leq C_{a}<\infty

and
b_{\theta}(x,\eta_{s}^{N})\leq C_{b}<\infty
,
and we have assumed that
h^{\prime}(u)\leq 0
(since
h
is nonincreasing),
so we may bound

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle h,\xi_{t}^{N}\rangle&\leq\langle h,\xi_{0}^{N}\rangle+M^{h}_{t}\\
&\qquad{}+\int_{0}^{t}\big\langle 2C_{a}\int_{u}^{\infty}h(v)dv+\left(C_{a}u^{2}+C_{b}u\right)|h^{\prime}(u)|,\xi_{s}^{N}(dx,du)\big\rangle ds.\end{split}

(8.5)

Now, since
\xi_{t}^{N}
is conditionally uniform given
\eta_{t}^{N}

in the sense of (
5.12
),
we know that for compactly supported
f
,

\mathbb{E}[\langle f,\xi^{N}_{t}\rangle]=\mathbb{E}[\langle\widetilde{f}_{N},\eta^{N}_{t}\rangle]
,
where
\widetilde{f}_{N}(x)=\int_{0}^{N}f(x,u)du
.
By our assumptions on
h
,
we know that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(2C_{a}\int_{u}^{\infty}h(v)dv+\left(C_{a}u^{2}+C_{b}u\right)|h^{\prime}(u)|\right)du<C

for some
C<\infty
, and so (by dominated convergence)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\langle h,\xi_{t}^{N}\rangle\right]&\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\langle h,\xi_{0}^{N}\rangle\right]+C\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\big\langle 1,\eta_{s}^{N}\big\rangle\bigg]ds,\end{split}

which we know by Lemma
6.1

is bounded by
C_{0}e^{C_{1}t}
for some other constants
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
.

Now consider the maximum. By (
8.5
),
using that the integrand is nonnegative,

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\langle h,\xi_{t}^{N}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle h,\xi_{0}^{N}\rangle+\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}M^{h}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\int_{0}^{T}\big\langle 2C_{a}\int_{u}^{\infty}h(v)dv+\left(C_{a}u^{2}+C_{b}u\right)|h^{\prime}(u)|,\xi_{s}^{N}(dx,du)\big\rangle ds.

As in the proof of Lemma
6.2
,
the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality,

Barlow et al. 1986
,
and the fact that
\sqrt{x}\leq 1+x
for
x\geq 0

tells us that there is a
C^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}M^{h}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\left(1+\mathbb{E}\left[\langle M^{h}\rangle_{T}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\left(1+\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\langle 2c_{\theta}(x,\eta_{s}^{N})\int_{u}^{\infty}h(v)^{2}dv,\xi_{s}^{N}(dx,du)\big\rangle\right]ds\right)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\left(1+2C_{a}\int_{0}^{\infty}h(v)^{2}dv\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\langle 1,\xi_{s}^{N}(dx,du)\big\rangle\right]ds\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}e^{C_{1}T},

for a constant
C_{2}
which is finite
by our assumption that
\int_{0}^{\infty}h(v)^{2}dv<\infty
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\langle h,\xi_{t}^{N}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\langle h,\xi_{0}^{N}\rangle\right]+(C_{2}+C_{0}/C_{1})e^{C_{1}T},

and so

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\langle h,\xi_{t}^{N}\rangle>K\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\langle h,\xi_{0}^{N}\rangle\right]+(C_{2}+C_{0}/C_{1})e^{C_{1}T}}{K}.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
f
be a bounded, continuous real-valued function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,\infty)

with uniformly bounded first and second derivatives
for which there exists a
u_{0}
such that if
u>u_{0}
then
f(x,u)=0
.
Then, the sequence of real-valued processes
(\langle f,\xi_{t}^{N}\rangle)_{t\geq 0}
for
N\geq 1

is tight in
{\cal D}_{[0,\infty)}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof of Lemma
8.5
:

Again, we use the Aldous-Rebolledo criterion.
Tightness of
\langle f,\xi_{t}\rangle
for a fixed
t

follows from Lemma
8.3
,
so we need only prove conditions analogous to
(
6.9
)
and (
6.10
)
applied to the martingale representation
of equations (
8.2
) and (
8.3
).
Rewriting (
8.2
) with
c_{\theta}=c_{\theta}(x,\eta_{s})
,

\displaystyle\langle f,\xi_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle f,\xi_{0}\rangle+M^{f}_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\Big\langle c_{\theta}\int_{u}^{N}\int(f(y,u_{1})+f(x,u_{1}))q^{m}(x,dy,\eta)du_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad+c_{\theta}(N-u)\int_{0}^{t}(f(y,u)-f(x,u))q^{m}(x,dy,\eta)+(c_{\theta}u^{2}-b_{\theta}u)\frac{d}{du}f,\xi_{s}\Big\rangle ds.

The bounds analogous to (
6.9
) and (
6.10
)
follow as in the proof of Lemma
6.3
:
for instance, observe that using that
c_{\theta}\leq C_{a}
for some
C_{a}
,
the predictable part of this semimartingale decomposition is bounded by

\displaystyle\Big\langle 2C_{a}\|f\|_{\infty}u_{f}+(1-u/N)\gamma B^{\theta}_{f}+(C_{a}u^{2}-b_{\theta}u)\frac{d}{du}f,\xi_{s}\Big\rangle,

the last term of which is bounded by

\displaystyle\langle C_{a}u_{f}^{2}+\sup_{x}|b_{\theta}(x,\eta_{s})|u_{f}\|\frac{d}{du}f\|_{\infty}\rangle,

which can be bounded as we did for (
6.9
).

8.2
Motion of ancestral lineages

In this section we prove Theorem
2.24
.
The argument follows directly from the discussion in Section
5.3
.

Proof of Theorem
2.24
:

For brevity, in the proof we write
\gamma(x)
or
\gamma
for
\gamma(x,\eta)
.

Here we have taken the high-density, deterministic limit
(so,
\theta,N\to\infty
and
\theta/N\to 0
).
We first proceed informally,
as if the limiting process has a density
\varphi_{t}(x)
at location
x
and time
t
(which it may not),
and follow this with an integration against test functions to make the argument rigorous.
Let
Y
denote the spatial motion followed by a single line of descent.
Above equation (
5.19
),
we showed that
Y
is a diffusion with generator at time
s

\mathcal{L}^{Y}_{s}g(x)=\gamma(x,\eta_{s})(\mathcal{B}(r(\cdot,\eta_{s})g(\cdot))(x)-g(x)\mathcal{B}r(x,\eta_{s})).

The diffusion is time-inhomogeneous if the density is not constant in time.
Let
\varphi_{t}(x)
be the limiting density,
which is a weak solution to (
1.1
),

\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}=r\mathcal{B}^{*}[\varphi_{t}\gamma]+\varphi_{t}F
.
Formally, the intensity of individuals at
y
at time
t

that are descended from individuals that were at
x
at time
s

(with
s<t
) is

\varphi_{s}(x)\mathbb{E}_{s,x}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}(F+\gamma\mathcal{B}r)(Y_{u})du\right)\mathbf{1}_{Y_{t}=y}\right]dy,

(8.6)

where the subscript
s,x
in the expectation indicates that
Y_{s}=x
.
To see why this should be true,
suppose that an ancestor at time
s
has level
v
. Conditional on its
spatial motion
\{Y_{u}\}_{s\leq u\leq t}
, its level at time
t
will
be
v\exp(-\int_{s}^{t}(F+\gamma\mathcal{B}r)(Y_{u})du)
. This will be less than a given level

\lambda
if
v<\lambda\exp(\int_{s}^{t}(F+\gamma\mathcal{B}r)(Y_{u})du)
.
The intensity of levels at
y
that are descended from individuals at

x
can therefore be obtained as the limit as
\lambda\to\infty
of

1/\lambda
times the number of levels at
x
at time
s
with

u<\lambda\exp(\int_{s}^{t}(F+\gamma\mathcal{B}r)(Y_{u})du)
and for which
the corresponding individual is at
y
at time
t
, which is
precisely the quantity in (
8.6
).

By our construction in Section
5.3
,
when we integrate (
8.6
)
with respect to
x
we recover
\varphi_{t}(y)dy
.
Consider an individual sampled at location
y
at time
t
,
and write
p(t,s,y,x)
for the probability density
that their ancestor at time
s
was at
x
.
As a consequence of (
8.6
),
still formally,

p(t,s,y,x)=\frac{\varphi_{s}(x)}{\varphi_{t}(y)}\mathbb{E}_{s,x}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}(F+\gamma\mathcal{B}r)(Y_{u})du\right)\mathbf{1}_{Y_{t}=y}\right]\qquad\text{ for }s<t.

(8.7)

To make (
8.7
) meaningful, we multiply by suitable test functions

f
and
g
and integrate.

\displaystyle\int\int f(y)\varphi_{t}(y)p(t,s,y,x)g(x)dydx

\displaystyle\qquad=\int g(x)\varphi_{s}(x)\mathbb{E}_{x,s}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}(F+\gamma\mathcal{B}r)(Y_{u})du\right)f(Y_{t})\right]dx.

Writing
\widehat{T}_{t,s}
for the time-inhomogeneous semigroup
corresponding to the motion of ancestral lineages backwards in time
(that is,
\widehat{T}_{t,s}f(y)=\int p(t,s,x,y)f(x)dy
),
we can write this as

\displaystyle\int f(y)\varphi_{t}(y)\widehat{T}_{t,s}g(y)dy=\int g(x)\varphi_{s}(x)\mathbb{E}_{s,x}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}(F+\gamma\mathcal{B}r)(Y_{u})du\right)f(Y_{t})\right]dx.

(8.8)

Next, we will differentiate this equation with respect to
t
.
There are two terms in the product on the left-hand side that depend on
t
,
so if we
use that
\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}=r\mathcal{B}^{*}[\varphi_{t}\gamma]+\varphi_{t}F

(in a weak sense),
and write
\mathcal{L}_{u}
for the generator of
\widehat{T}_{t,s}
at time
t=u

so that
\partial_{t}\widehat{T}_{t,s}g(y)\Big|_{t=s}=\mathcal{L}_{s}g(y)
,
then

\qquad\qquad\frac{d}{dt}\int f(y)\varphi_{t}(y)\widehat{T}_{t,s}g(y)dy\Big|_{t=s}\\
{}=\int f(y)\left\{\varphi_{s}(y)\mathcal{L}_{s}g(y)+\left[r(y)\mathcal{B}^{*}(\gamma\varphi_{s})(y)+\varphi_{s}(y)F(y)\right]g(y)\right\}dy.

As for the right-hand side, since
Y_{s}=x
under
\mathbb{E}_{x,s}
,

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{x,s}\left[\exp\left(\int_{s}^{t}(F+\gamma\mathcal{B}r)(Y_{u})du\right)f(Y_{t})\right]\Bigg|_{t=s}=\left[F(x)+\gamma(x)\mathcal{B}r(x)\right]f(x)+\mathcal{L}^{Y}_{s}f(x).

Therefore, the derivative of (
8.8
)
(with respect to
t
, evaluated at
t=s
) is

\displaystyle\int f(y)\left\{\varphi_{s}(y)\mathcal{L}_{s}g(y)+\left(r(y)\mathcal{B}^{*}(\gamma\varphi_{s})(y)+\varphi_{s}(y)F(y)\right)g(y)\right\}dy

\displaystyle\qquad=\int g(x)\varphi_{s}(x)\left(\mathcal{L}^{Y}_{s}f(x)+\left[F(x)+\gamma(x)\mathcal{B}r(x)\right]f(x)\right)dx

\displaystyle\qquad=\int f(x)\left((\mathcal{L}^{Y}_{s})^{*}(\varphi_{s}g)(x)+\left[F(x)+\gamma(x)\mathcal{B}r(x)\right]\varphi_{s}(x)g(x)\right)dx,

where
(\mathcal{L}^{Y}_{s})^{*}
is the adjoint of
\mathcal{L}^{Y}_{s}
.
Since
f
was arbitrary,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{s}g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varphi_{s}}\left[(\mathcal{L}^{Y}_{s})^{*}(\varphi_{s}g)+\gamma\varphi_{s}g\mathcal{B}(r)-rg\mathcal{B}^{*}(\gamma\varphi_{s})\right].

(Note that the
\varphi_{s}Fg
terms have cancelled.)
Since the adjoint of
{\mathcal{L}}^{Y}_{s}
is

\displaystyle({\mathcal{L}}^{Y}_{s})^{*}f

\displaystyle=r\mathcal{B}^{*}(\gamma f)-\gamma f\mathcal{B}r,

we can rewrite the generator of a lineage as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{s}g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r}{\varphi_{s}}\left[\mathcal{B}^{*}(\gamma\varphi_{s}g)-g\mathcal{B}^{*}(\gamma\varphi_{s})\right].

This is equation (
2.17
).

To simplify to equation (
2.18
),
first define

\mathcal{D}f(x)=\sum_{ij}\mathbf{C}_{ij}\partial_{ij}f(x),

and so the adjoint of
\mathcal{D}
is

\mathcal{D}^{*}f(x)=\sum_{ij}\partial_{ij}(\mathbf{C}_{ij}f(x)).

Note that
\mathcal{D}^{*}
satisfies the following identity:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{*}(fg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}\left\{g\partial_{ij}(\mathbf{C}_{ij}f)+2f\partial_{i}(\mathbf{C}_{ij})\partial_{j}(g)+2\mathbf{C}_{ij}\partial_{i}(f)\partial_{j}(g)+\mathbf{C}_{ij}f\partial_{ij}g\right\}

\displaystyle=g\mathcal{D}^{*}f+2f\vec{c}\cdot\nabla g+2(\mathbf{C}\nabla f)\cdot\nabla g+f\mathcal{D}g,

where
\vec{c}_{j}=\sum_{i}\partial_{i}\mathbf{C}_{ij}
.
So, with
f=\gamma\varphi_{s}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{s}g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r}{\varphi_{s}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}^{*}(\gamma\varphi_{s}g)-\nabla\cdot(\gamma\varphi_{s}g\vec{b})-\frac{1}{2}g\mathcal{D}^{*}(\gamma\varphi_{s})+g\nabla\cdot(\gamma\varphi_{s}\vec{b})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r}{\varphi_{s}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\gamma\varphi_{s}\mathcal{D}g+\gamma\varphi_{s}\vec{c}\cdot\nabla g+(\mathbf{C}\nabla(\gamma\varphi_{s}))\cdot\nabla g-\gamma\varphi_{s}\vec{b}\cdot\nabla g\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\gamma\left[\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}g+\vec{c}\cdot\nabla g+(\mathbf{C}\nabla\log(\gamma\varphi_{s}))\cdot\nabla g-\vec{b}\cdot\nabla g\right],

which is equation (
2.18
).

Proof of Corollary
2.27
:

For the moment, we will write
r(x)
for
r(x,\eta)

and
\gamma(x)
for
\gamma(x,\eta)
.
First note that
since in this case the semigroup does not depend on time,
we can write
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{s}
, and

\mathcal{L}f=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}r\gamma\left(\Delta f+\nabla(2\log(\gamma\varphi)-2h/\sigma^{2})\cdot\nabla f\right).

Now, observe that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{H(x)}f(x)(\Delta+\nabla H(x)\cdot\nabla)g(x)dx=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{H(x)}\left\{\nabla f(x)\cdot\nabla g(x)\right\}dx,

so that by choosing
H(x)=2\log(\gamma(x)\varphi(x))-2h(x)/\sigma^{2}

and

\pi(x)=\frac{e^{H(x)}}{\sigma^{2}r(x)\gamma(x)/2}=\frac{\gamma(x)\varphi(x)^{2}e^{-2h(x)/\sigma^{2}}}{\sigma^{2}r(x)/2},

we have that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pi(x)f(x)\mathcal{L}g(x)dx=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{H(x)}\nabla f(x)\cdot\nabla g(x)dx.

Since this Dirichlet form is symmetric in
f
and
g
,
the process
Y
is reversible with respect to
\pi

(and the factor of
\sigma^{2}/2
is constant).

Appendix A
Markov Mapping Theorem

The following appears as Theorem A.2 in
Etheridge and Kurtz 2019
,
specialized slightly here to the case that the processes
are càdlàg and have no fixed points of discontinuity.
For an
S_{0}
-valued, measurable process
Y
,
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{Y}_{t}

denotes the completion of the
\sigma
-algebra generated by

Y(0)
and
\{\int_{0}^{r}h(Y(s))ds,r\leq t,h\in B(S_{0})\}
.
Also, let
D_{S}[0,\infty)
denote the space of càdlàg,
S
-valued functions
with the Skorohod topology, and
M_{S}[0,\infty)
the space of Borel measurable functions
from
[0,\infty)
to
S
,
topologized by convergence in Lesbegue measure.
For other definitions see
Etheridge and Kurtz 2019
.

Theorem A.1
(Markov Mapping Theorem).

Let
(S,d)
and
(S_{0},d_{0})
be complete, separable metric spaces.
Let
A\subset C_{b}(S)\times C(S)
and
\psi\in C(S)
,
\psi\geq 1
.
Suppose that for each
f\in\mathcal{D}(A)
there exists
c_{f}

such that

|Af(x)|\leq c_{f}\psi(x),\qquad x\in A,

and define
A_{0}f(x)=Af(x)/\psi(x)
.

Suppose that
A_{0}
is a countably determined pre-generator,
and suppose that
\mathcal{D}(A)=\mathcal{D}(A_{0})

is closed under multiplication and is separating.
Let
\gamma:S\to S_{0}
be Borel measurable,
and let
\alpha
be a transition function from
S_{0}
into
S

(
y\in S_{0}\to\alpha(y,\cdot)\in\mathcal{P}(S)
is Borel measurable)
satisfying
\int h\circ\gamma(x)\alpha(y,dx)=h(y)
for
y\in S_{0}
and
h\in B(S_{0})
,
that is,
\alpha(y,\gamma^{-1}(y))=1
.
Assume that
\widetilde{\psi}(y)\equiv\int_{S}\psi(z)\alpha(y,dz)<\infty
for each
y\in S_{0}

and define

\displaystyle C=\{\int_{S}f(z)\alpha(\cdot,dz),\int_{S}Af(z)\alpha(\cdot,dz)\;:\;f\in\mathcal{D}(A)\}.

(A.1)

Let
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(S_{0})
and define
\nu_{0}=\int\alpha(y,\cdot)\mu_{0}(dy)
.

(a)

If
\widetilde{Y}
satisfies
\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\psi}(\widetilde{Y}(s))]ds<\infty

for all
t\geq 0
and
\widetilde{Y}
is a solution of the martingale problem for
(C,\mu_{0})
,
then there exists a solution
X
of the martingale problem for
(A,\nu_{0})

such that
\widetilde{Y}
has the same distribution on
M_{S_{0}}[0,\infty)

as
Y=\gamma\circ X
.
If
Y
and
\widetilde{Y}
are càdlàg, then
Y
and
\widetilde{Y}
have the same distribution
on
D_{S_{0}}[0,\infty)
.

(b)

For
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}\{X(t)\in\Gamma\;|\;\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{Y}_{t}\}=\alpha(Y(t),\Gamma),\qquad\text{for }\Gamma\in\mathcal{B}(S).

(c)

If, in addition, uniqueness holds for the martingale problem for
(A,\nu_{0})
,
then uniqueness holds for the
M_{S_{0}}[0,\infty)
-martingale problem for
(C,\mu_{0})
.
If
\widetilde{Y}
has sample paths in
D_{S_{0}}[0,\infty)
then uniqueness holds for the

D_{S_{0}}[0,\infty)
-martingale problem for
(C,\mu_{0})
.

(d)

If uniqueness holds for the martingale problem for
(A,\nu_{0})

then
Y
is a Markov process.

In our application, we have taken
S
to be the space of locally finite counting measures
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,N)
or on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,\infty)
,
and
S_{0}
the space of finite measures on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Then,
A
corresponds to the generator for the lookdown process (i.e., either
A^{N}
or
A
),
and
C
corresponds to the generator for the spatial population process
(i.e., either
\mathcal{P}^{N}
or
\mathcal{P}
).
The “
\gamma
” of the theorem is our spatial projection operator
that we have called
\kappa^{N}
or
\kappa
,
and the “
\alpha
” of the theorem will be named
\Gamma^{N}
or
\Gamma
below.
Finally, “
X
” of the theorem is our lookdown process,
\xi
,
and “
Y
” is our spatial process,
\eta
.

A.1
Lookdown Generators

In this section we verify one of the conditions of the Markov Mapping Theorem,
namely, that “integrating out levels” in the generator of the lookdown process
we obtain the generator of the projected process.
In the notation of the theorem,
we are verifying that
C
defined in (
A.1
) is in fact
\mathcal{P}^{N}

(if defined with
A^{N}
) or
\mathcal{P}^{\infty}
(if defined with
A
).
We will work with test functions of the form

f(\xi)=\prod_{(x,u)\in\xi}g(x,u)=\exp\left(\langle\log g,\xi\rangle\right),

(A.2)

where
0\leq g\leq 1
and
g(x,u)=1
for all
u\geq u_{g}
for some
u_{g}<\infty
.
Furthermore, recall that
\kappa^{N}(\xi)(\cdot)=\xi(\cdot\times[0,N))/N

is the “spatial projection operator”,
and define the transition function
\Gamma^{N}:\mathcal{M}_{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,N))

so that for
\eta\in\mathcal{M}_{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, if
\widehat{g}_{N}(x)=\int_{0}^{N}g(x,u)du/N
, then

\displaystyle F^{N}_{g}(\eta)

\displaystyle:=\int f(\xi)\Gamma^{N}(\eta,d\xi)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(N\left\langle\log\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{N}g(x,u)du,\eta(dx)\right\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(N\left\langle\log\widehat{g}_{N}(x),\eta(dx)\right\rangle\right),

i.e.,
\Gamma^{N}
assigns independent labels on
[0,N]
to each of the points in
\eta
.
It follows from Lemma
6.5
that for test functions of this form
the generator of
\eta^{N}_{t}
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{P}^{N}F^{N}_{g}(\eta)=F^{N}_{g}(\eta)N\theta&\bigg\langle\gamma(x,\eta)\int r(z,\eta)\left(\widehat{g}_{N}(z)-1\right)q_{\theta}(x,dz)\\
&\qquad\qquad{}+\mu_{\theta}(x,\eta)\left(\frac{1}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}-1\right),\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle.\end{split}

(A.3)

(Note that
f
here differs from the
f
used in Lemma
6.5

so as to agree with standard usage in the literature on lookdown processes.)
The generator of
\xi^{N}_{t}
is
A^{N}
, defined in equation (
5.10
).

Lemma A.2
.

For all finite counting measures
\eta
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
if
f
is of the form (
A.2
), then

\displaystyle\int A^{N}f(\xi)\Gamma^{N}(\eta,d\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{N}F^{N}_{g}(\eta).

(A.4)

For the limiting process,
recall that
\kappa(\xi)(\cdot)=\lim_{u\to\infty}\xi(\cdot\times[0,u))/u

is the “spatial projection operator”,
and define the probability kernel
\Gamma:\mathcal{M}_{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,\infty))

so that for
\eta\in\mathcal{M}_{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
defining
\widetilde{g}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}(g(x,u)-1)du
,

\displaystyle F_{g}(\eta)

\displaystyle:=\int f(\xi)\Gamma(\eta,d\xi)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\big\langle\int_{0}^{\infty}(g(x,u)-1)du,\eta(dx)\big\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=e^{\langle\widetilde{g}(x),\eta(dx)\rangle}.

i.e.,
\Gamma(\eta,\cdot)
is the distribution of a conditionally Poisson process
with intensity a product of
\eta
and Lebesgue measure.
It again follows from Lemma
6.5
that for test functions of this form
the generator of
\eta_{t}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\infty}F_{g}(\eta)

\displaystyle=F_{g}(\eta)\left\langle\gamma(x,\eta)\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{g}(\cdot)r(\cdot))(x)+F(x,\eta)\widetilde{g}(x)+\alpha\gamma(x,\eta)r(x,\eta)\widetilde{g}^{2}(x),\eta(dx)\right\rangle.

(A.5)

The generator of
\xi_{t}
is
A
, defined in equation (
5.11
).

Lemma A.3
.

For all
\eta\in\mathcal{M}_{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
if
f
is of the form (
A.2
), then

\displaystyle\int Af(\xi)\Gamma(\eta,d\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}^{\infty}F_{g}(\eta).

(A.6)

Proof of Lemma
A.2
:

First, break the generator
A^{N}
into three parts,

\displaystyle A^{N}_{1}f(\xi)

\displaystyle=f(\xi)\sum_{(x,u)\in\xi}2c_{\theta}(x,\eta)\int_{u}^{N}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}\frac{g(x,v_{1})}{g(x,u)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(g(y,u)-g(x,u))q^{m}_{\theta}(x,dy,\eta)\Bigg)dv_{1},

\displaystyle A^{N}_{2}f(\xi)

\displaystyle=f(\xi)\sum_{(x,u)\in\xi}2c_{\theta}(x,\eta)\int_{u}^{N}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\frac{g(y,v_{1})+g(x,v_{1})}{2}-1\right)q^{m}_{\theta}(x,dy,\eta)\Bigg)dv_{1},

\displaystyle A^{N}_{3}f(\xi)

\displaystyle=f(\xi)\sum_{(x,u)\in\xi}\,\left(c_{\theta}(x,\eta)u^{2}-b_{\theta}(x,\eta)u\right)\frac{\partial_{u}g(x,u)}{g(x,u)},

where
q^{m}
was defined in equation (
8.1
),
so that

A^{N}f(\xi)=A^{N}_{1}f(\xi)+A^{N}_{2}f(\xi)+A^{N}_{3}f(\xi).

We now integrate each piece against
\Gamma^{N}
.
First note that by the product form of
f
,

\int f(\xi)\sum_{(x,u)\in\xi}\frac{\ell(x,u)}{g(x,u)}\Gamma^{N}(\eta,d\xi)=F_{g}^{N}(\eta)\bigg\langle\frac{1}{N\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}\int_{0}^{N}\ell(x,u)du,N\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int A^{N}_{1}f(\xi)\Gamma^{N}(\eta,d\xi)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt=F_{g}^{N}(\eta)\bigg\langle\frac{c_{\theta}(x,\eta)}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}\int_{0}^{N}\Bigg\{\int_{u}^{N}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}g(x,v_{1})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(g(y,u)-g(x,u))q^{m}_{\theta}(x,dy,\eta)\Bigg)dv_{1}\Bigg\}du,\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt=F_{g}^{N}(\eta)\bigg\langle\frac{c_{\theta}(x,\eta)}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}\Bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{\int_{0}^{N}\int_{u}^{N}g(x,v_{1})(g(y,u)-g(x,u))dv_{1}du\right\}q^{m}_{\theta}(x,dy,\eta),\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle.

For the second generator, we have

\displaystyle\int A^{N}_{2}f(\xi)\Gamma^{N}(\eta,d\xi)

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt=F_{g}^{N}(\eta)\bigg\langle\frac{c_{\theta}(x,\eta)}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}\int_{0}^{N}g(x,u)\Bigg\{\int_{u}^{N}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\frac{g(y,v_{1})+g(x,v_{1})}{2}-1\right)q^{m}_{\theta}(x,dy,\eta)\Bigg)dv_{1}\Bigg\},\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt=F_{g}^{N}(\eta)\bigg\langle\frac{c_{\theta}(x,\eta)}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigg\{\int_{0}^{N}\int_{u}^{N}g(x,u)\left(g(y,v_{1})+g(x,v_{1})-2\right)dv_{1}du\Bigg\}q^{m}_{\theta}(x,dy,\eta),\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle.

For the third generator we have that

\displaystyle\int A^{N}_{3}f(\xi)\Gamma^{N}(\eta,d\xi)

\displaystyle\qquad=F_{g}^{N}(\eta)\bigg\langle\frac{1}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}\int_{0}^{N}\left(c_{\theta}(x,\eta)u^{2}-b_{\theta}(x,\eta)u\right)\partial_{u}g(x,u)du,\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad=F_{g}^{N}(\eta)\bigg\langle\frac{1}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}\int_{0}^{N}\left(b_{\theta}(x,\eta)-2c_{\theta}(x,\eta)u\right)(g(x,u)-1)du,\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle.

Note that
2\int_{0}^{N}\int_{u}^{N}g(x,v_{1})g(y,u)dv_{1}du=N^{2}\widehat{g}_{N}(x)\widehat{g}_{N}(y)
,
and so

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{N}\int_{u}^{N}g(x,v_{1})(g(y,u)-g(x,u))dv_{1}du+\int_{0}^{N}\int_{u}^{N}g(x,u)\left(g(y,v_{1})+g(x,v_{1})-2\right)dv_{1}du

\displaystyle\qquad=N^{2}\widehat{g_{N}}(x)(\widehat{g_{N}}(y)-2)+2\int_{0}^{N}ug(x,u)du.

Combining the last equations,
and using the fact that

Nc_{\theta}(x,\eta)-b_{\theta}(x,\eta)=\theta\mu_{\theta}(x,\eta)
,
we have

\displaystyle\int\Bigg(A^{N}_{1}f(\xi)+A^{N}_{2}f(\xi)+A^{N}_{3}f(\xi)\Bigg)\Gamma^{N}(\eta,d\xi)

\displaystyle\qquad=F_{g}^{N}(\eta)\bigg\langle c_{\theta}(x,\eta)N^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\widehat{g}_{N}(y)-2)q^{m}(x,dy,\eta)+\frac{1}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}c_{\theta}(x,\eta)\int_{0}^{N}2udu

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad{}+\frac{1}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}b_{\theta}(x,\eta)\int_{0}^{N}(g(x,u)-1)du,\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad=F_{g}^{N}(\eta)\bigg\langle c_{\theta}(x,\eta)N^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\widehat{g}_{N}(y)-1)q^{m}(x,dy,\eta)+N^{2}c_{\theta}(x,\eta)\left(\frac{1}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}-1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad{}+Nb_{\theta}(x,\eta)\left(1-\frac{1}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}\right),\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad=F_{g}^{N}(\eta)N\bigg\langle Nc_{\theta}(x,\eta)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\widehat{g}_{N}(y)-1)q^{m}(x,dy,\eta)+\theta\mu_{\theta}(x,\eta)\left(\frac{1}{\widehat{g}_{N}(x)}-1\right),\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle.

This matches equation (
A.3
), as desired,
because
Nc_{\theta}(x,\eta)q_{m}(x,dy,\eta)=\theta\gamma(x,\eta)q_{\theta}(x,dy)
.

Before proving Lemma
A.3
,
we recall an important equality for conditionally
Poisson point processes (
Kurtz and Rodrigues 2011
Lemma A.3).

Lemma A.4
.

If
\xi=\sum_{i}\delta_{Z_{i}}
is a Poisson random measure with mean measure
\nu
,
then for
\ell\in L^{1}(\nu)
and
g\geq 0
with
\log g\in L^{1}(\nu)
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j}\ell(Z_{j})\prod_{i}g(Z_{i})\right]=\int\ell gd\nu e^{\int(g-1)d\nu}.

(A.7)

Proof of Lemma
A.3
:

By Lemma
A.4
,

\int f(\xi)\sum_{(x,u)\in\xi}\frac{\ell(x,u)}{g(x,u)}\Gamma(\eta,\xi)=F_{g}(\eta)\bigg\langle\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell(x,u)du,\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle.

Comparing this to the definition of
A

(equation (
5.11
)),
we see that

\displaystyle\int Af(\xi)\Gamma(\eta,d\xi)

\displaystyle=F_{g}(\eta)\bigg\langle\int_{0}^{\infty}(\ell_{1}(x,u)+\ell_{2}(x,u)+\ell_{3}(x,u))du,\eta(dx)\bigg\rangle,

where

\displaystyle\ell_{1}(x,u)

\displaystyle=\gamma(x,\eta)\left(\mathcal{B}(g(\cdot,u)r(\cdot,\eta))(x)-g(x,u)\mathcal{B}r(x,\eta)\right)

\displaystyle=\gamma(x,\eta)\left(\mathcal{B}((g(\cdot,u)-1)r(\cdot,\eta))(x)-(g(x,u)-1)\mathcal{B}r(x,\eta)\right)

and

\displaystyle\ell_{2}(x,u)=2g(x,u)\alpha\gamma(x,\eta)r(x,\eta)\int_{u}^{\infty}(g(x,v)-1)dv

and

\displaystyle\ell_{3}(x,u)=\left(\alpha\gamma(x,\eta)r(x,\eta)u^{2}-\{\gamma(x,\eta)\mathcal{B}r(x,\eta)+F(x,\eta)\}u\right)\partial_{u}g(x,u).

First note that since
\mathcal{B}
acts on space, it commutes with the integral over levels, and so

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell_{1}(x,u)du

\displaystyle=\gamma(x,\eta)\left(\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{g}(\cdot)r(\cdot,\eta))(x)-\widetilde{g}(x)\mathcal{B}r(x,\eta)\right),

since
\widetilde{g}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}(g(x,u)-1)du
.
Next,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell_{2}(x,u)du

\displaystyle=\alpha\gamma(x,\eta)r(x,\eta)2\int_{0}^{\infty}g(x,u)\int_{u}^{\infty}(g(x,v)-1)dvdu.

Finally, integrating by parts,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\ell_{3}(x,u)du

\displaystyle=-\alpha\gamma(x,\eta)r(x,\eta)\int_{0}^{\infty}2u(g(x,u)-1)du

\displaystyle\qquad{}+\{\gamma(x,\eta)\mathcal{B}r(x,\eta)+F(x,\eta)\}\widetilde{g}(x)

Now, note that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}g(x,u)\int_{u}^{\infty}(g(x,v)-1)dvdu-\int_{0}^{\infty}u(g(x,u)-1)du

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{0}^{\infty}g(x,u)\int_{u}^{\infty}(g(x,v)-1)dvdu-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{v}^{\infty}(g(x,u)-1)dudv

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{0}^{\infty}(g(x,u)-1)\int_{u}^{\infty}(g(x,v)-1)dvdu

\displaystyle\qquad=\widetilde{g}(x)^{2}/2.

Adding these together, we get that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(\ell_{1}(x,u)+\ell_{2}(x,u)+\ell_{3}(x,u))du

\displaystyle\qquad=\gamma(x,\eta)\mathcal{B}(\widetilde{g}(\cdot)r(\cdot,\eta))(x)+F(x,\eta)\widetilde{g}(x)+\alpha\gamma(x,\eta)r(x,\eta)\widetilde{g}(x)^{2},

which agrees with (
A.5
), as desired.

Appendix B
Technical Lemmas

B.1
Constraints on kernel widths

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose the first three conditions of Assumptions
2.8
hold,
and furthermore the kernels
\rho_{r}=p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}
and
\rho_{\gamma}=p_{\epsilon_{\gamma}^{2}}

are each Gaussian with standard deviations
\epsilon_{r}
and
\epsilon_{\gamma}
respectively.
Let
\lambda=\sup_{x}\sup_{y:\|y\|=1}y^{T}\mathbf{C}(x)y
be the largest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{C}(x)

across all
x
.
If
\epsilon_{r}^{2}+\frac{2\lambda}{\theta}<\epsilon_{\gamma}^{2}
, then
there is a
C<\infty
such that
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\eta\in\mathcal{M}_{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\left|\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\rho_{r}\!*\!\eta(y)-\rho_{r}\!*\!\eta(x))q_{\theta}(x,dy)\right|\leq C\rho_{\gamma}\!*\!\eta(x)

(B.1)

and

\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho_{r}\!*\!\eta(y)-\rho_{r}\!*\!\eta(x)\right)^{2}q_{\theta}(x,dy)\leq C\left(\rho_{\gamma}\!*\!\eta(x)\right)^{2}.

(B.2)

Note that the right hand side of each is the average density
over a wider region (since
\epsilon_{\gamma}>\epsilon_{r}
).
The key assumption here
is that the spatial scale over which local density affects birth rate
is larger than the scale over which it affects establishment.
In the simple case of
\vec{b}=0
and
\mathbf{C}=\sigma^{2}I
,
the condition is simply that
\epsilon_{r}^{2}+2\sigma^{2}/\theta<\epsilon_{\gamma}^{2}
.
This gives a yet more concrete situation in which
Condition
2
of Lemma
2.9
holds.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
:

First we prove (
B.1
).
Recall that
\rho_{r}\!*\!\eta(x)=\int p_{\epsilon^{2}_{r}}(x-w)\eta(dw)
,
where
p_{t}
is the density of a Gaussian with mean 0 and variance
t
,
so that applying Fubini, (
B.1
) is

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(y-w)-p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(x-w))q_{\theta}(x,dy)\eta(dw)\right|.

Write
p_{s,x}(\cdot)
for the density of a Gaussian
with mean
s\vec{b}(x)
and covariance
\epsilon_{r}^{2}I+s\mathbf{C}(x)
,
so that
\int p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(y-w)q_{\theta}(x,dy)=p_{1/\theta,x}(w-x)
.
It therefore suffices to show that for all
x
and
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
there exists
K
such that

\displaystyle\left|\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(y-w)-p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(x-w))q_{\theta}(x,dy)\right|

\displaystyle=\theta\left|p_{1/\theta,x}(w-x)-p_{0,x}(w-x)\right|\leq Kp_{\epsilon^{2}_{\gamma}}(w-x).

However,

\theta(p_{1/\theta,x}(z)-p_{0,x}(z))=\partial_{s}p_{s,x}(z)
for some
0\leq s\leq 1/\theta
.
Write
\Gamma(s,x)=\epsilon_{r}^{2}I+s\mathbf{C}(x)
,
so that

\displaystyle p_{s,x}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left(2\pi|\Gamma(s,x)|\right)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}(z-s\vec{b}(x))^{T}\Gamma(s,x)^{-1}(z-s\vec{b}(x))\right),

and note that if
\lambda_{i}
are the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{C}(x)

then
|\Gamma(s,x)|=\prod_{i}(\epsilon_{r}^{2}+s\lambda_{i})
,
and

\partial_{s}|\Gamma(s,x)|=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}|\Gamma(s,x)|/(\epsilon_{r}^{2}+s\lambda_{i})
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\partial_{s}p_{s,x}(z)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\vec{b}(x)^{T}\Gamma(s,x)^{-1}(z-s\vec{b}(x))+(z-s\vec{b}(x))^{T}\Gamma(s,x)^{-1}\mathbf{C}(x)\Gamma(s,x)^{-1}(z-s\vec{b}(x))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad{}-\sum_{i}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\epsilon_{r}^{2}+s\lambda_{i}}\bigg)p_{s,x}(z),

where
z^{T}
is the transpose of
z
.
This implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(y-w)-p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(x-w))q_{\theta}(x,dy)}{p_{\epsilon^{2}_{\gamma}}(x-w).}=h(x-w)e^{k(x-w)},

where
h(z)
and
k(z)
are quadratic polynomials in
z

whose coefficients depend on
s
and
x
but are uniformly bounded,
and

\displaystyle k(z)=\frac{1}{2\epsilon_{\gamma}^{2}}\|z\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}(z-s\vec{b}(x))^{T}\Gamma(s,x)^{-1}(z-s\vec{b}(x)),

Since
\inf_{z}z^{T}\Gamma(s,x)^{-1}z/\|z\|^{2}=1/(s\lambda(x)+\epsilon_{r}^{2})
,
where
\lambda(x)=\sup z^{T}\mathbf{C}(x)z/\|z\|^{2}
is the largest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{C}(x)
,
this is negative for all
z
outside a bounded region, and so
equation (
B.1
) follows from the assumption
that
\epsilon_{r}^{2}+2\sup_{x}\lambda(x)/\theta<\epsilon_{\gamma}^{2}
.
(Note that we do not yet need the factor of 2.)

Next we prove equation (
B.2
), in a similar way.
Again applying Fubini,

\displaystyle\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\rho_{r}\!*\!\eta(y)-\rho_{r}\!*\!\eta(x)\right)^{2}q_{\theta}(x,dy)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(y-w)-p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(x-w))(p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(y-v)-p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(x-v))q_{\theta}(x,dy)\eta(dv)\eta(dw),

and so as before, equation (
B.2
) will follow if
the integrand is bounded by
Kp_{\gamma}(x-w)p_{\gamma}(x-v)
.
Now, let
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
, and
Z
be independent
d
-dimensional Gaussians with mean zero,
where
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
have covariance
\epsilon^{2}_{r}I
,
and
Z
has covariance
\mathbf{C}(x)
.
Write
p_{s,t,x}(\cdot,\cdot)
for the joint density of

Y_{1}+\sqrt{s}Z+s\vec{b}(x)
and
Y_{2}+\sqrt{t}Z+t\vec{b}(x)
.
Then, observe that

\displaystyle\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(y-w)-p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(x-w))(p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(y-v)-p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(x-v))q_{\theta}(x,dy)

\displaystyle\qquad=\theta\left(p_{1/\theta,1/\theta,x}(x-w,x-v)-p_{0,1/\theta,x}(x-w,x-v)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.{}-p_{1/\theta,0,x}(x-w,x-v)+p_{0,0,x}(x-w,x-v)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\partial_{s}p_{s,1/\theta,x}(x-w,x-v)-\partial_{t}p_{0,t,x}(x-w,x-v),

for some
0\leq s,t\leq 1/\theta
.
As before,

\displaystyle\frac{\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(y-w)-p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(x-w))(p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(y-v)-p_{\epsilon_{r}^{2}}(x-v))q_{\theta}(x,dy)}{p_{\epsilon_{\gamma}^{2}}(x-w)p_{\epsilon_{\gamma}^{2}}(x-v)}=h(x-w,x-v)e^{k(x-w,x-v)},

where
h(z_{1},z_{2})
is a polynomial with uniformly bounded coefficients and

\displaystyle k(z_{1},z_{2})=(\|z_{1}\|^{2}+\|z_{2}\|^{2})/(2\epsilon_{\gamma}^{2})-\frac{1}{2}[z_{1},z_{2}]^{T}\Gamma(s,t,x)^{-1}[z_{1},z_{2}],

where
[z_{1},z_{2}]
is the
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
vector formed by concatenating
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
,
and
\Gamma(s,t,x)
is the block matrix

\displaystyle\Gamma(s,t,x)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\epsilon_{r}^{2}I+s\mathbf{C}(x)&\sqrt{st}\mathbf{C}(x)\\
\sqrt{st}\mathbf{C}(x)&\epsilon_{r}^{2}I+t\mathbf{C}(x)\\
\end{array}\right].

If
\mathbf{C}(x)u=au
for some
a\in\mathbb{R}
,
then
[u\sqrt{s},u\sqrt{t}]
is an eigenvector of
\Gamma(s,t,x)

with eigenvalue
\epsilon^{2}_{r}+(s+t)a
,
and
[u\sqrt{t},-u\sqrt{s}]
is an eigenvector of
\Gamma(s,t,x)

with eigenvalue 0.
This implies the largest eigenvalue of
\Gamma(s,t,x)

is equal to
\epsilon^{2}_{r}+(s+t)\lambda(x)
,
where
\lambda(x)
is again the largest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{C}(x)
.
Therefore, if
s+t\leq 2/\theta
,

\displaystyle(\|z_{1}\|^{2}+\|z_{2}\|^{2})/\epsilon^{2}_{\gamma}-[z_{1},z_{2}]^{T}\Gamma(s,t,x)^{-1}[z_{1},z_{2}]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(\|z_{1}\|^{2}+\|z_{2}\|^{2})\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}_{\gamma}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}_{r}+2\lambda(x)/\theta}\right),

which is negative by assumption.
Therefore, there is a
K
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\partial_{s}p_{s,1/\theta,x}(x-w,x-v)-\partial_{t}p_{0,t,x}(x-w,x-v)\right|}{p_{\epsilon^{2}_{\gamma}}(x-w)p_{\epsilon^{2}_{\gamma}}(x-v)}\leq K

for all
\theta>1
and all
x
,
v
, and
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
proving equation (
B.2
) and hence the lemma.

B.2
Tightness of processes

Here we record, for completeness, the fact used above
that tightness for a family of processes, if determined by the Aldous-Rebolledo criterion,
extends to sums and products of those processes.
We first record for reference
one version of the Aldous-Rebolledo criteria for tightness of a sequence real-valued processes
(as it appears in Theorem 1.17 of
Etheridge 2000
;
see also Theorem 4.13 of
Jacod and Shiryaev 2013
):

Theorem B.2
(
Rebolledo 1980
).

Let
\{Y^{(n)}\}_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of real-valued processes
with càdlàg paths.
Suppose that the following conditions are satisfied.

1.

For each fixed
t\in[0,T]
,
\{Y_{t}^{(n)}\}_{n\geq 1}
is tight.

2.

Given a sequence of stopping times
\tau_{n}
, bounded by
T
,
for each
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
and
n_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq n_{0}}\sup_{\theta\in[0,\min(\delta,T-\tau_{n})]}\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}-Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|>\epsilon\right\}\leq\epsilon.

Then the sequence
\{(Y^{(n)}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}\}_{n\geq 1}
is tight.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\{X^{(n)}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\{Y^{(n)}\}_{n\geq 1}
be sequences of jointly defined
real-valued processes with càdlàg paths
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
B.2
.
Then
\{X^{(n)}Y^{(n)}\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\{X^{(n)}+Y^{(n)}\}_{n\geq 1}
also
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
B.2
.

By “jointly defined” we mean that
X^{(n)}
and
Y^{(n)}
are defined on the same
probability space, so that the products and sums make sense.

Proof of Lemma
B.3
:

The proof for
X^{(n)}+Y^{(n)}
is similar to but more straightforward than
for
X^{(n)}Y^{(n)}
, so on only prove the Lemma for the latter.

First, note that for any
\epsilon>0
, by tightness of
(X^{(n)}_{t})_{n\geq 0}
and
(Y^{(n)}_{t})_{n\geq 0}

there is a
K
such that

\mathbb{P}\{X^{(n)}_{t}>\sqrt{K}\}
and
\mathbb{P}\{Y^{(n)}_{t}>\sqrt{K}\}

are both less than
\epsilon/2
,
and hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{X^{(n)}_{t}Y^{(n)}_{t}>K\}\leq\mathbb{P}\{X^{(n)}_{t}>\sqrt{K}\}+\mathbb{P}\{Y^{(n)}_{t}>\sqrt{K}\}\leq\epsilon.

Therefore,
(X^{(n)}_{t}Y^{(n)}_{t})_{n\geq 0}
is tight.

Next, note that for
0\leq\tau_{n}\leq T
,

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq\min(\delta,T-\tau_{n})}\left|X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}-X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq\min(\delta,T-\tau_{n})}\left|X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}\right|\left|Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}-Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|+\left|X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}-X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|\left|Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left|X^{(n)}_{t}\right|\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq\min(\delta,T-\tau_{n})}\left|Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}-Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|+\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq\min(\delta,T-\tau_{n})}\left|X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}-X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left|Y^{(n)}_{t}\right|,

so that for any
C
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq\min(\delta,T-\tau_{n})}\left|X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}-X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|>\epsilon\right\}\\
&\qquad\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left|X^{(n)}_{t}\right|>C\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq\min(\delta,T-\tau_{n})}\left|Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}-Y^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|>\epsilon/C\right\}\\
&\qquad{}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq\min(\delta,T-\tau_{n})}\left|X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}-X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|>\epsilon/C\right\}+\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left|Y^{(n)}_{t}\right|>C\right\}\end{split}

(B.3)

Now, since
\max_{0\leq t\leq T}X^{(n)}_{t}
is tight (and likewise for
Y
)
(see, e.g., Remark 3.7.3 in
Ethier and Kurtz 1986
),
we may choose a
C\geq 4
for which

\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left|X^{(n)}_{t}\right|>C\right\}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}.

Similarly, by assumption we can choose a
\delta
for which

\mathbb{P}\left\{\sup_{0\leq\theta\leq\min(\delta,T-\tau_{n})}\left|X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}+\theta}-X^{(n)}_{\tau_{n}}\right|>\epsilon/C\right\}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{C}.

If we choose
C
and
\delta
that do this for both
X^{(n)}
and
Y^{(n)}
,
then each of the terms in equation (
B.3
) are bounded by
\epsilon/4
,
and condition (2) is satisfied for the product process.
